The limits span 766-830 MMACs, an 8% band, while the feature footprint at the limit falls 2.4x
across the same rows. One output-channel group never fails at any reachable height, because its
work reaches only 548 MMACs, under the band. The capacity rule `feature + weights <= pool` does
not fit at all. One group is exact at a feature-plus-cube total of 6888 granules, where two
groups fail at 5992.

#### Work and output size

The deadline limits the program's execution time, not the size of the output it writes
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]. The six rows above hold `ic` and `k` fixed, where the MACs
are exactly proportional to the output elements. A work law and an output-bytes law fit them
equally. Five further test points break that proportionality through the kernel, the plane
width and `ic`, and they separate the two laws by factors:

| Test point | ic | oc | iw | k | first wrong height | output there | work there |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `k7g2-112` | 32 | 64 | 112 | 7 | 79 | 495 KB | 777 MMAC |
| `k7g3-112` | 32 | 96 | 112 | 7 | 57 | 519 KB | 814 MMAC |
| `k7g4-112` | 32 | 128 | 112 | 7 | 45 | 529 KB | 830 MMAC |
| `k7g5-112` | 32 | 160 | 112 | 7 | 37 | 526 KB | 824 MMAC |
| `k7g6-112` | 32 | 192 | 112 | 7 | 33 | 550 KB | 862 MMAC |
| `k5g4-112` | 32 | 128 | 112 | 5 | 77 | 1009 KB | 807 MMAC |
| `k7g4-224` | 32 | 128 | 224 | 7 | 25 | 530 KB | 831 MMAC |
| `k7g4-56` | 32 | 128 | 56 | 7 | 60, dense from 89 | 346 KB, 531 KB | 542, 833 MMAC |
| `k7ic64-g2` | 64 | 64 | 112 | 7 | 34 | 190 KB | 596 MMAC |
| `k3g4-112` | 32 | 128 | 112 | 3 | none to 109 | 1507 KB exact | 434 MMAC |
| `k7g1-112` | 32 | 32 | 112 | 7 | none to 109 | 349 KB exact | 548 MMAC |

Output size does not set the limit. At the first failures, output spans 190 KB to 1009 KB, a
factor of 5.3, where work spans 542-862 MMACs, a factor of 1.6. The test point `k3g4-112` writes
1507 KB with no error at any reachable height, while `k7ic64-g2` is wrong at 190 KB, eight times
less. At three taps the MACs per output element fall 5.4x and the output grows, and the limit
disappears.

Output rows do not set it either. The test point `k7g4-224` is wrong at 19 output rows, and
`k7g4-56` is exact past 78. A row count fitted on the `112`-wide family, 38 output rows, misses
in both directions by about a factor of two. The width moves the limit through the total work,
not through the row count.

Work stands in for the program's execution time, and does so well only where the weight traffic
per output is fixed. The five `112`-wide k7 test points and `k7g4-224` fall in 777-862 MMACs,
and the dense region of `k7g4-56` starts at 833. The test point `k7ic64-g2` fails 27% below
that band, at 596 MMACs. It has twice the resident weight slice per output-channel group (1568
granules against 784), so each output element costs more DMA than a MAC count sees. The
explanation of the residual spread is [hypothesis], and the measured result is the ordering.

At 786 MHz, 800 MMACs in 500 us is about 2050 MACs per cycle, and two cores at that rate make
the RK3576's marketed 6 TOPS. A one-task program's whole compute therefore fits inside the
grace.

At `k7ic64-g2` the failure changes form at height 37, where two mechanisms meet. Below 37 the
wrong elements are a row tail of the last output-channel group, the deadline's form. From 37 up
they are every row of that group (rows 0-30 of 31), and the planner's CBUF `F` moves from 0 to
1024 at the same height.

On a `rocket` module that waits for the writing block, the heights below 37 are exact. Heights
37-45 stay wrong at exactly the same element counts under both modules (104936 at 37, 108489 at
38, 111936 at 39). The second mechanism is therefore deterministic and independent of timing,
and [The weight slice and the feature allowance](#the-weight-slice-and-the-feature-allowance)
describes it. Software support:
`librocketnpu` refuses the heights from 37, because the slice cap's `F`-aware form does not
cover them.

#### Failure rate below the refused heights

At heights 33 to 36 on `k7ic64-g2`, below the refused heights, the failures are the drain
deadline [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]. The planner programs `F=0` there. The plane needs
3696 to 4032 of 4096 granules, and the 98 KiB slice is inside the 156 KiB `F=0` cap. These
heights are wrong on `rocket` 1.5.0 at its default grace, and bit-exact under either of two
independent removals of the deadline:

| Module and grace | ih 33 | ih 34 | ih 35 | ih 36 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `rocket` 1.5.0, `dpu_grace_us` 500 | 2 of 30 | 14 of 60 | 26 of 60 | 56 of 60 |
| `rocket` 1.5.0, `dpu_grace_us` 1000 | n/a | 0 of 30 | 0 of 30 | 0 of 30 |
| `rocket` 1.6.0, which waits for the writing block, at its default | 0 of 30 | 0 of 30 | 0 of 30 | 0 of 30 |

> [!NOTE]
> At a fixed height, the failure rate varies widely between identical runs. At ih 35, six runs
> of the same command at the same grace gave 0, 1, 1, 6, 8 and 10 wrong of 10 repetitions. A
> run with no failures does not show that a height is safe. Nor does the range of heights a
> single run marks as failing.

#### Extent of the loss

The extent of the loss depends on when the fence falls, along one continuum
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]. A late cut drops the row tail of one output-channel group:
hundreds to a few thousand wrong elements, `chans 32-47`. An earlier cut leaves the output wrong
across every row and all 64 channels, 150000 to 170000 of 203520 elements. Sweeping
`dpu_grace_us` at `k7ic64-g2` heights 33-36 moves the extent smoothly from one form to the
other:

| `dpu_grace_us` | wrong reps | wrong elements per rep | channel span | row span |
|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| 500 | 11 of 40 | 1184 - 3808 | `c 32-47` | a 1-3 row suffix |
| 250 | 40 of 40 | 27798 - 68768 | `c 32-63` | from row 5-16 down |
| 100 | 40 of 40 | 84638 - 131008 | `c 0-63` | every row |
| 0 | 40 of 40 | 116364 - 155008 | `c 0-63` | every row |

Grace 0 at ih 36 reaches 155008 elements, inside the severe form's band. The severe form at
grace 500 is therefore the tail of one distribution, not a second event. Of the 131 wrong
repetitions in the sweep, 107 fall between the two forms. At grace 500 the severe form did not
occur once in 240 repetitions on 2026-08-05.

Every wrong element is a dropped write, never a wrong value [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05].
Each output is stamped with `0xA5` before the submit. Every wrong element in every wrong
repetition still holds the stamp, and no wrong element holds data. The result holds at every
grace value, including 0, where the driver retires each job as early as it can. A lost drain
therefore costs part of the job's own output, and the core does not write into an unrelated
job's buffer. The result holds for one process issuing sequential convolution submits ~10 ms
apart, at one geometry.

**Not characterized:** a victim in another process, of another job type, or in a graph
submitting back to back.

The loss grows in the order `c 32-47`, then `c 48-63`, then `c 0-31`, never in natural channel
order, at any grace value [HW sweep]. The likely cause is the DPU's DDR write order, in which
32-47 is the last channel group this geometry writes [hypothesis].

> [!NOTE]
> A slice-cap overrun and the drain deadline both leave output unwritten, and the shape of the
> output alone does not separate them. The stamped elements do, by where they fall. A slice-cap
> overrun loses whole trailing output-channel groups, every row of each: `chans 32-63`, exactly
> 50% here. The deadline loses a suffix in write order, at whatever depth the cut fell.

### The pooling divisor lag

A padded average pool intermittently divides an output window by the tap count of the previous
window in raster order. The sum is right, and only the divisor is one output position late. The
predecessor of a row's first window is the previous row's last window. Once the lag starts, it
persists to the last channel group of the program (see "Lag extent and the tail-only check"
below). A failing output is therefore a contiguous suffix of channel groups, wrong at a few
columns of every row and exact everywhere else.

> [!CAUTION]
> A padded average pool in exclude-pad mode can divide an output window by the previous window's
> tap count, intermittently and with no fault. The output is written in full, and is wrong. Software
> must check the positions whose raster predecessor has a different tap count, and redo the submit
> on a mismatch.

The lag is observable only where the pad is excluded, the only mode whose divisor varies with
position. At a constant divisor, the previous window's divisor is this window's. The lag can
therefore occur under every max pool and every count-include-pad average on the RK3576 without
changing the output.

The lag is independent of the wide-output hazard. An int32-output matmul in front leaves its rate
where it was, and the lag reproduces standalone with nothing before it at all. A redo without a
power cycle repairs it, since a domain collapse is neither necessary nor sufficient for this
hazard. Its rate depends on the composition of buffers in the process. The same shape and entry
range between 0% and 95% with no program changing (see "What sets the loud/quiet rate" below).

The lag can show only at positions whose raster predecessor has a different tap count. For a SAME
window, those are two columns of every row, plus a position where the vertical count changes. A
check of them costs three columns: O(rows) per image rather than O(rows × columns). Any occurrence
shows at thousands of them at once.

In a graph the rate is about a third of calls. In Inception V3, `librocketnpu` repairs five to
eleven calls per inference set across the graph's nine padded average pools. A failing output has
30-40% of its observable elements wrong. A run of failures is therefore ordinary, and is not a sign
of another fault.

##### Software support

`librocketnpu` checks the observable positions after every padded average pool and redoes the
submit on a failure, with no power cycle. The check also covers a cube join, which is where the
hazard costs a label. A joined pool reads the producer's output rather than a row-major tensor:
the same values, in the layout the PPU itself fetched them from. The check is the same arithmetic
over a different accessor, and it costs one cache-maintenance bracket the join otherwise never
incurs. Only a padded average pool incurs it.

A cross-layer kick issues the submit instead of the entry, so the kick runs the same check from its
verify bracket (`rocket_pool_int8_rk3576_lag_check()`). It runs before the write check, because
that loop re-stamps as it goes and the pool's producer is earlier in the same chained run. The
check first asks whether an output was written at all. An unwritten output goes to the write check,
which has the power cycle this hazard does not need.

The redos have their own budget of 32, `ROCKET_RK3576_POOL_LAG_ATTEMPTS`, separate from the
wide-output hazard's eight. A lag redo costs a submit, where a wide-output redo costs a power cycle.
A kick also redoes every pool of the chained run at once. Two observable pools in one stream must therefore
both be clean on the same attempt.

#### What sets the loud/quiet rate

The divisor lag's rate depends on where the job's buffers fall in the IOVA space, modulo 64 KiB.
A pool fires on a call whose output shows the lag. A loud configuration fires on most calls, and a
quiet one on almost none.

The same geometry, entry and binary, called back to back, range between ~0% and ~95% depending on
which other buffers the process allocated. A BO allocated before the job's buffers, and never
submitted against or touched, moves the rate. The rate follows a map periodic in exactly 16 pages,
64 KiB [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]. A loud band is a run of adjacent loud residues of that
period. `rocket` exposes no way to read an IOVA back. The 64 KiB modulus is therefore measured
through the allocation offset, and the mechanism behind it is unidentified.

The rule has five measured properties [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]:

- The variable is the total page count allocated before the job's own buffers, not the buffer
  count. `rocket_bo_alloc` rounds to 4096, so a byte-valued rule is not even expressible.
- Freeing the earlier allocation restores the unpadded state exactly. It leaves no pressure, no
  fragmentation and no page-table residue behind.
- The period is a constant of the address space, and its phase is absolute. Which residues are loud
  is a function of the geometry and the entry.
- The prepacked cube-in path obeys the same 64 KiB period. It needs both a phase and other pool
  handles present in the process. Those handles need not run. A `rocket_bo_alloc` that no program
  ever names shifts the phase, but cannot create a loud band.
- Only a susceptible pool at its own phase is loud. In a process of fifteen pool configurations,
  two show loud bands, at disjoint phases, and the other thirteen show none at any phase.

What makes a configuration susceptible is not identified, and it is not a gradient in the source
line stride. A source line stride of 128 gives a loud band, and the next value down, 64, gives none
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-06]. The phase is not predictable from a byte count, because some
allocation in the bookkeeping is uncounted [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05].

> [!NOTE]
> A divisor-lag rate measured for one shape holds only for the buffer composition it was measured
> in. A standalone test of one shape does not give that shape's rate in a graph. A pad sweep run on
> a shape in isolation reads quiet at all sixteen phases.

#### Exposure and cost in the model corpus

One model of the seven-model corpus is exposed to this hazard at all. The check runs only where
the pad is excluded from an average. A max pool and a count-include-pad average have a constant
divisor and cannot show the lag. The table counts each model's own pooling calls:

| model | pooling calls an inference | observable (padded exclude-pad average) |
|---|---:|---:|
| Inception V3 | 10 | **9** |
| Inception V1, MobileNet V1, MobileNet V2, ResNet-18 | n/a | **0** |
| EfficientDet-Lite0 | 14 | **0** (all max) |
| SSD MobileNet V2 | 0 | **0** |

Inception V3 sits on a band's partial edge, neither in it nor out of it. Eight of its nine
observable pools fire on 0-9% of calls, the pad sweep's quiet level. Layer 125 (`2048x8x8`, cube in
and cube out) fires on 22.9-33.1%, the rate the pad sweep produces at a band's partial residue. The
graph is reproducibly one phase step from a rate three times higher, and one step from nothing.
Which of the three applies is not predictable from a byte count.

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, Inception V3, `bench 100`, `ondemand` governor, 2026-08-06.

The check and its redos together cost Inception V3 1.5 ms of 34.0 ms (4.4%). The redos are ~1
extra submit an inference [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-06]. The check is 1.04 ms an inference,
incurred on every call the hazard can show on, and a redo is incurred only at the hazard's rate. A
pool call measures 283 us clean and 562 us with one redo.

A setting that moved the graph into a quiet phase would remove the redo half only. The redo half is
~0.5 ms of 34.0 ms, ~1.5% on one model of seven, and zero on the other six [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
2026-08-06]. `librocketnpu` keeps the check, because a phase that cannot be predicted cannot be
trusted.

> [!NOTE]
> Profiling with `ROCKET_RK3576_POOL_PROF=1` roughly halves the lag's rate. The per-call host work
> between submits is itself part of the composition the rate depends on. Use the profile for the
> per-redo cost, and an uninstrumented run for the rate.

Every failing element is the previous window's divisor, and none is anything else. The check scores
rather than judges. It counts the observable elements that match the right divisor, those that
match the raster predecessor's, and those that match neither. A zero third count identifies a
failure as this hazard rather than a defect in the accessor.

#### Coverage cost per graph

The divisor-lag check costs nothing on four graphs of five. Only a padded average pool has a
position where the divisor varies, and only Inception V3 has one. The other four measure identical
wall-clock times with the check on and off. Inception V3's cost is under "Exposure and cost in
the model corpus".

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, `bench 100`, with `ROCKET_RK3576_POOL_LAGCHECK=0` as the control,
2026-08-03.

| graph | wall-clock time, check on or off (ms) |
|---|---:|
| MobileNetV1 | 5.0 |
| MobileNetV2 | 6.5-6.6 |
| ResNet-18 | 6.6 |
| Inception V1 | 7.0-7.1 |

Placing a padded average pool inside a chained kick raises the graph's time and makes it vary from
run to run. The spread is the redos, whose count is a random variable. The placement moves both
halves of the cost, and `librocketnpu` refuses such a pool as a member of a chained run (see "Chained-run refusal for
pools whose divisor can lag" below).

The check and the redos are separate costs that share a loop position and nothing else, and the
check is the larger one. The check is arithmetic plus a cache-maintenance bracket, incurred on
every call the hazard can show on, so it barely moves with the placement. It is host work rather
than NPU time. A redo is incurred at the hazard's rate, and what the placement moves is what each
redo costs. A redo inside a kick re-runs every program of the chained run.

#### The pooling path's read interval is three things, and only two of them are the guard

The pooling path's `read` interval runs from the post-submit `PREP_BO` to the closing `FINI_BO`. It
contains the fence wait as well as the write check, and the wait is the PPU finishing, not a guard.
The profile under `ROCKET_RK3576_POOL_PROF=1` prints the split as wait / scan / fini.

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, Inception V3, `bench 100`, `ondemand` governor, 2026-08-03, per
inference over 15.2 pool calls.

| term | ms | what it is |
|---|---:|---|
| divisor lag check | 5.98 | host arithmetic over the observable positions |
| fence wait | 2.85 | the PPU finishing, plus one invalidate of the output |
| de-tile | 1.44 | the column-split pools' output side |
| sentinel stamp | 1.05 | prep + memset + fini, per call |
| redos | 0.81 | every attempt after the first |
| closing fini | 0.38 | the guard's other half |
| submit ioctl | 0.25 | |
| host cube scatter | 0.07 | |
| sentinel scan | **0.00** | it stops at the first byte the program wrote |

The tail-only check (see "Lag extent and the tail-only check") takes the lag check's figure to
about 1 ms.

The pooling write guard is `stamp + scan + fini` = 1.43 ms [host-computed from the table]. The
fence wait inside the `read` interval is not part of it, and the sentinel scan costs nothing
measurable.

The guard is zero on the other four graphs. Their pools are members of chained runs, so the per-call pooling
entry never runs at all.

#### Lag check cost

The lag check is a pass over the source cube and the output, not arithmetic. It touches ~60% of the
cache lines of both, at roughly 0.5 GB/s of effective line traffic. No reordering helps, because
the positions it must score are spread over every row.

`librocketnpu`'s check scores 16 channels at a time rather than one. An atom is sixteen consecutive
channels, so a per-channel walk is sixteen strided passes over the same lines. Two further
optimizations are each byte-identical, and neither trades coverage:

- **The tap loop in NEON.** On a cube the sixteen lanes of an atom are sixteen contiguous bytes, so
  a tap is one 16-byte load and two widening adds.
- **The rounding's three integer divisions, hoisted out of the element loop.** The quotient and the
  two modulos behind `exact_recip` are functions of the output position, not of the channel. They
  are built once per position, and the quotient becomes an exact reciprocal multiply,
  `M = 2^32/n + 1`. The multiply is exact for every window a pool can have, because the argument
  is under 2^16.

#### Lag extent and the tail-only check

The lag is a contiguous suffix of channel groups that ends at the last channel group. Its onset
varies, and is near-constant per shape: group 7 of 48, 44 or 108 of 128, 1 or 5 of 18. The end never
varies. The PPU's divisor state therefore slips once and does not resynchronize inside a program.

The onset's constancy holds at one memory-system load. A change in that load moves the onset (see
"Memory traffic moves the onset and the rate, the other way" below). The end does not move: under an
applied DRAM load every occurrence still reaches the last group.

##### Software support

`librocketnpu` scores the last channel group alone. Scoring it detects every occurrence recorded, at
1/ngroups of the cost, and its detection is indistinguishable from the full check's.

> [!NOTE]
> The tail-only check rests on an observation of how the PPU's divisor state fails, not on a
> register. A lag that stopped short of the last group would be missed. It would leave a full-size,
> wrong output that no redo count or passing test shows. `ROCKET_RK3576_POOL_LAG_TAIL=0` restores
> the full scan.

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, Inception V3, `bench 100`, 2026-08-04.

On the default `ondemand` governor the tail-only check takes Inception V3's wall-clock time from
38.2 to 33.4 ms (12.6%). With the governor pinned to `performance`, where the configurations'
differing host work cannot move the clock, the saving is 3.53 ms. The saving is zero on the other
four networks [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]. Their pools are members of chained runs, so the per-call
entry never runs.

#### Lag rate per layer in Inception V3

The rate is not a function of the geometry. In Inception V3, the four 768×17×17 branch pools have
the same shape, program and channel count. They fire at 49, 0, 3 and 6 percent.

Every one of the nine padded average pools has thirteen to thirty-seven thousand elements whose two
candidate divisors give different values on its data. A pool that does not fire is therefore not an
artifact of rounding.

Run standalone, the same shapes give different rates from those in the graph, and two of the three
differ in opposite directions. The 17×17×768 pool fires on 0 of 60 calls standalone and 49%
in the graph at the same geometry. No function of the geometry fits.

The rate does not depend on the gap between submits, on the source's channel-group stride, or on
the input data.

#### Lag rate and submit structure

The rate depends on whether the pool's program runs inside a chained kick or as its own submit
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]. The two configurations below have the same handle, the same register
program, the same source cube at the same BO, and the same data.

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, Inception V3, `bench 100`, three repeats a configuration.

| layer | shape | inside a kick | its own submit |
|---|---|---:|---:|
| 110 | 1280×8×8 | 52.6 / 35.7 / 46.7 % | **1.0 / 0.0 / 0.0 %** |
| 125 | 2048×8×8 | 51.2 / 82.1 / 61.9 % | 35.7 / 8.2 / 22.9 % |

The graph's own layers show the same direction. Layer 50 reads a source the host scattered
immediately before the submit, and fires at 49%. Layers 62, 74 and 86 have the same shape and the
same program. They read a cube that an earlier, completed submit wrote, and fire at 0-15%.

The two high-rate configurations share a source written shortly before the PPU reads it
[hypothesis]. In one, the preceding program of the same job writes it microseconds earlier. In the
other, the host writes it between the scatter and the submit ioctl. The low-rate configuration is
separated from its producer by a whole fence. The hypothesis fits every measurement above, and does
not explain the standalone probe. Its source is also host-written immediately before, and it fires
on 0 of 60 calls at one shape and 55 of 60 at another.

**Not characterized:** the mechanism. Only the kick against own-submit comparison is measured.

#### Memory traffic moves the onset and the rate, the other way

CPU-side DRAM traffic lowers the lag's rate at a loud shape and spreads its onset
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-05]. The traffic is applied from the CPU, touching no NPU buffer and
issuing no ioctl. Memory-system pressure is not what separates loud shapes from quiet ones:

- **The quiet shape stays quiet.** `768x17x17` does not reach 0.2% of calls standalone at any load.
  Pressure does not create the hazard.
- **The loud shape moves the other way, with DRAM traffic and not with core occupancy.** At
  `288x35x35` the rate falls from 92.6% idle to 79.2% under 8.3-8.4 GB/s of copy traffic. A spin
  control on the same cores with no memory traffic sits above idle, at 95.6%. Less DRAM traffic
  means more lag.
- **The onset is a run-time property, not a constant of the shape.** Idle, it is a point mass on
  group 2. Loaded, it spreads across groups g0-g6.

The slip is tied to how fast the PPU's feature stream arrives relative to its own divisor sequencing
[hypothesis]. Slowing delivery then both delays the slip and makes it less likely, which fits all
three readings. It is not a decode, and it does not name what separates loud shapes from quiet ones.

**Not characterized:** pressure applied from the NPU side rather than the CPU's, and anything about
the physical BO or IOVA. The measurement covers one SoC, one pooling mode and three shapes.
#### Chained-run refusal for pools whose divisor can lag

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, Inception V3, 2026-08-03, with the divisor-lag check scoring every
channel group (before the tail-only check).

**Not characterized:** a graph whose lag-prone pool sits in a long chained run at a low lag rate. There
the split would cost two submits and save nothing. The measurements cover two pools on one
graph.

A pool whose divisor can lag must not be a member of a chained run. Software must
give it its own submit. A kick is one submit, so a lag redo inside one is joint: it re-runs
every program of the chained run. Every observable pool in the chained run must then come out clean on the same
attempt. The redo budget is per hazard, and a joint attempt uses it up geometrically.

With Inception V3's two padded average pools inside one 41-layer kick, the per-attempt joint
clean rate falls to about 0.12. The default budget of 32 redos then runs out. The result is 8
hard failures in 1100 inferences, and none with each pool in its own submit [HW sweep, H96 MAX
M9]. A run that exhausts the budget ends with `the surface was never right` and returns
`ROCKET_E_DEVICE`.

`rocket_chain_plan_rk3576()` refuses such a pool as a member of a chained run, rather than raising the budget.
The lag rate itself moves the same way inside a kick, and is the larger half of the failure. A
larger budget would only cover a hazard that the placement creates.

The refusal applies only to pools that can lag. A max pool, an unpadded average pool and a
count-include-pad average pool have no position where the divisor varies. They stay members of
chained runs. A `conv -> pool -> conv` sequence in one stream is unaffected. The refusal reads the
geometry rather than `_lag_observable()`, so that `ROCKET_RK3576_POOL_LAGCHECK=0` isolates the
check's cost without also changing the chained-run structure. `ROCKET_RK3576_CHAIN_POOL_LAG=1` puts
such a pool back in a chained run, for testing.

The refusal costs nothing. Inception V3 splits its 89-129 chained run into 89-109, 114-118 and 119-124.
With the refusal it measures 40.7-41.9 ms, against 46.9-62.8 ms with one such pool back in a
kick: faster, and with the spread gone. The joint redos are most of the variance: with the pool
in the kick, an inference runs 173-293 tasks against 132. Both ranges are measured with the
column-split pools joined cube-in. The other four graphs have no padded average pool, and the
refusal does not change them.

A lower lag rate does not remove the hazard. Software must keep the lag check and the redo
budget for a pool in its own submit.

#### Divisor lag on the cube-in path

The divisor lag also occurs when a padded average pool reads a cube that an earlier submit
wrote. It occurs with one submit per layer and no kick.

> [!CAUTION]
> Without a lag check on the cube-in path, the cube-chained Inception V3 disagrees with the
> row-major lowering at the classifier. It differs in 831-963 of 1001 elements. Software must
> apply the divisor lag check on the cube-in path as well as the row-major one.

The cause is the divisor lag, and neither the kick nor the joins. Holding the divisor constant
across positions (`ROCKET_RK3576_NET_AVGNOPAD=0`) removes the disagreement, with every join,
every program and every chained-run boundary unchanged. With the check extended to the cube-in path,
the cube-chained Inception V3 passes its network test.

> [!NOTE]
> The lag is intermittent. A sweep with one run per test point, such as one over the kick size,
> can read as a race. Repeat each test point before reading a non-monotone result as one.

### Unresolved hazards and limits

#### Drain deadline

On `rocket` 1.5.0, a program whose DPU drain outlasts `dpu_grace_us` loses its output tail
(RK3576-E3). [The direct path's drain deadline](#the-direct-paths-drain-deadline) states the
hazard and its scope by module version.
[The drain deadline and the row planner](#the-drain-deadline-and-the-row-planner) states the
planner's agreement at a longer grace and the one-task case.

#### Pooling state across processes

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, a k3 s2 max pool at 16 channels as the next process's first pooling
submits, 2026-08-02.

A pooling program with a unit kernel on one axis changes the NPU's state for the next process.
The next process's first two pooling submits return incorrect output, and the state clears
itself after two pooling submits [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-02]. The failure follows
unit-kernel pooling programs, and not a wide-output int32 program or no program at all.

> [!CAUTION]
> After a pooling program with a unit kernel on one axis, the next process's first two pooling
> submits return incorrect output. The output has the correct size, no error is reported, and
> the failure is byte-identical every time. On a 16×16 plane, 128 elements are wrong, one output
> row across all sixteen channels. On a 24×24 plane, 2302 of 2304 elements are wrong. Software
> must not rely on the first two pooling outputs of a process that follows a unit-kernel pooling
> program, and must verify them.

Pooling with a unit kernel on one axis is also unstable in its own output.

**Not characterized:** which register or state contains the corruption, and whether a
non-pooling submit in between clears it. Whether that instability and this state are one defect
is not characterized, nor whether any geometry a real graph runs can produce it.

#### A pool at the start or end of a chained run

`librocketnpu` refuses a pool at the start or the end of a chained run. A pool that began a chained run
would need the chain to scatter a row-major tensor into its cube. One that ended a chained run would
need its output de-tiled. Both are host work the stream exists to remove, so the limit is
the library's rather than the hardware's. No measured graph needs it: both MobileNets and
ResNet-18 pool in the middle.

**Not characterized:** a pool at the start or the end of a chained run.

## Performance

### Cost of the library entries

Each public RK3576 entry with a CPU reference is 2.1-90.6x faster than a single-threaded scalar
CPU loop over the same tensors.

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, kernel 7.1.7 with `rocket` 1.6.0 in-tree, `dpu_grace_us` 0, the
`ondemand` governor, the driver's default 50 ms autosuspend delay, measured 2026-08-10.

The NPU column is the whole public entry, the cost a frontend sees. It covers host packing into
the cubes, the submits and the de-tile back to row-major, and it is not a kernel time. The
CPU column is a single-threaded scalar direct loop over the same tensors, auto-vectorized at the
library's own optimization level. It has no im2col, no packing and no threading, so it is a
reference rather than a tuned kernel.

Each ratio is therefore an upper bound against a tuned kernel. A good NEON GEMM across the four
A72s would close much of every one of them. Rows vary 20-40% run to run. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

| Shape | NPU ms | CPU ref ms | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| stem 3×224×224 k3 s2 oc32, int8 | 3.21 | 41.7 | 13.0x |
| stem 3×224×224 k3 s2 oc64, int8 | 4.62 | 83.1 | 18.0x |
| stem 3×224×224 k7 s2 oc32, int8 | 3.12 | 188.8 | 60.6x |
| stem 3×224×224 k3 s2 oc32, fp16 | 13.98 | n/a | n/a |
| conv 32->64 k1 56×56 | 2.41 | 12.0 | 5.0x |
| conv 64->64 k3 56×56 | 2.68 | 242.8 | 90.6x |
| conv 128->128 k3 28×28 | 2.52 | 186.9 | 74.3x |
| conv 256->256 k3 14×14 | 4.44 | 185.0 | 41.7x |
| conv 256->512 k1 14×14 | 1.87 | 52.3 | 28.0x |
| depthwise 64 k3 56×56 | 2.36 | 7.5 | 3.2x |
| depthwise 128 k3 28×28 | 1.42 | 4.0 | 2.9x |
| depthwise 256 k3 14×14 | 1.07 | 2.2 | 2.1x |
| matmul 32×1024×1024 | 4.32 | 13.3 | 3.1x |
| matmul 32×1024×2560 | 9.78 | 31.1 | 3.2x |
| matmul 128×1024×1024 | 5.20 | 52.8 | 10.2x |
| matmul 256×512×2048 | 7.12 | 106.5 | 15.0x |

The table shows four results at this operating point:

- **Submit cost.** A submit costs about 340 us (§"Per-submit overhead and the completion poll"),
  and the submit-bound rows track it.
- **Depthwise.** Depthwise convolution is 2.1-3.2x faster. Against a threaded CPU kernel it
  would be close. A depthwise convolution has `ic` times fewer MACs than a direct one at the
  same shape. The same per-submit cost therefore gives `ic` times less work. Batching several
  depthwise layers into one submit is the optimization the encoding would have to supply.
- **Matmul.** N is the axis that sets matmul throughput, and the planner already uses it. At M=32
  the planner puts K=N=1024 in a single submit, the largest N tile the RK3576 takes. The matmul
  at low M is therefore not dispatch-bound. Its limit was the host cube scatter, below.
- **Stem.** The quantized stem is the one to run, but the gap is 3.21 ms against 13.98, not
  against 226. Driving the power cycle the fp16 path's guard waits for closes the gap, not
  arithmetic. The remainder is one real cycle per call. The fp16 programs raise their own DPU
  completion on `rocket` 1.6.0 (§"Retirement on the DPU completion"), but at this measurement
  `librocketnpu` submitted them under `ROCKET_JOB_NO_DPU_DONE`. Each fp16 job therefore fenced
  at the `dpu_blind_us` settle past `PC_DONE`, not on that completion. Without the flag, as the
  library submits them, an fp16 job fences on its own completion.

#### The host cube scatter

The host cube scatter packs row-major tensors into the RK3576's cubes and de-tiles the
results back. Done an element at a time through the index functions, it costs a divide, a modulo
and a call per byte. Done that way, it takes most of a call's wall-clock time, more than the
submit. At
K=N=1024 that is a million of them, and twelve of that matmul's thirteen milliseconds.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]

Three of the scatters are block copies once the index is written out. At `kh=kw=1`,
`weight_conv_int8()` puts thirty-two consecutive contraction elements at thirty-two consecutive
bytes on both sides, and `feature_data()` at C2=16 does the same for sixteen. The matmul's
feature pack, weight pack and output de-tile are therefore blocked memcpys. The convolution
entries stay a real transpose, CHW against NC1HWC2, with a strided store, and their per-element
division hoists out to one pair per channel. The cost table above is measured with the block
copies.

The convolution entries' weight side has two more host loops of this kind. Both are functions of
the weights alone, and done per element, each walks a million elements through index arithmetic.
Only a whole graph, not a single shape, shows them:

- The per-output-channel filter sums, a contiguous scan written as a triple-nested index
  expression.
- The weight cube scatter. Its `taps == 1` case is thirty-two contiguous source bytes going to
  thirty-two contiguous cube bytes, a run of block moves rather than byte stores.

Straightening both takes MobileNetV1's graph from 146 ms to 115 ms, with every layer still
bit-exact [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-29]. Setting `ROCKET_RK3576_INT8_PROF=1` splits a
call's time into its operand packs, its submits and its de-tile.

A pixel-outer, channel-inner feature scatter is slower on the RK3576. It fills each C2-byte atom
in one go, and touches every destination line once instead of once per channel. It needs C2
concurrent read streams, though. On the graph it costs the depthwise layers more than it saves
the 1×1 ones: layer 3's feature pack goes from 3.2 to 5.3 ms. The scatter stays channel-outer,
with one sequential read stream and a strided store.

#### The int8 matmul entry

On the RK3576 an int8 matmul is a 1×1 convolution, which the convolution's register program runs
unchanged. Its M axis, its contraction width, its throughput along N and its two output-channel
bounds are in [rk3576-regcmd.md §"The matmul as a 1×1
convolution"](rk3576-regcmd.md#the-matmul-as-a-11-convolution). Two properties differ from the
RK3588:

- **Precision.** One int8 task contracts `ic*kh*kw` up to 4608, and one task of the fp16
  convolution program contracts sixteen input channels. A matmul lowered onto that fp16 program
  therefore costs 30-299x the int8 one. The matmul-form fp16 program contracts the whole K in one
  task, and at a prefill's M costs about twice the int8 matmul (§"The precision fork"). On the
  RK3588 fp16 is faster, and int8 saves only RAM.
- **Output.** The output is int8, through the DPU's requant, where the RK3588's int8 matmul writes
  raw int32 and sums K partials on the host. Inside one task's contraction the requant is the
  DPU's own, which is the fast path. A K with no single-task plan, `K >= 6176`, is declined
  (§"The K-split wedge").

`rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576()` is the userspace layer around the convolution. It is a separate
entry, not a path behind `rocket_matmul_int8()`, because of the output difference. Its work is:

- The operand scatter into the RK3576's cubes
- The output de-tile
- The tiling planner
- The per-chip dispatch point

Its CBUF planner refuses both output-channel bounds. The default tile of 2048 sits far enough
inside them to act as a guard. A CPU model holds the entry bit-exact over 33 shapes.

#### The int8 GEMM against a real CPU GEMM, at LLM prefill shapes

Against a tuned CPU GEMM at the shapes an LLM prefill issues, `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576()` is
3.3-6.8x faster. The median is 5.4x over the six shapes with correct output.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-08-05]

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, measured 2026-08-05. `M` is the ubatch, never 1. The NPU column is
the whole public entry: the host pack of A and B into the cubes, the submits and the de-tile.
The CPU column is the fastest of ggml's F32 `MUL_MAT`, ggml's Q8_0 `MUL_MAT` and OpenBLAS
`cblas_sgemm`, on four threads on the A72s.

| M / K / N | NPU ms | best CPU ms | ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 512 / 2048 / 512 | 10.29 | 34.39 | 3.34x |
| 512 / 2048 / 2048 | 28.20 | 133.22 | 4.72x |
| 1024 / 2048 / 2048 | 48.50 | 267.79 | 5.52x |
| 2048 / 2048 / 2048 | 78.70 | 532.24 | 6.76x |
| 512 / 2048 / 8192 | 91.07 | 533.27 | 5.86x |
| 512 / 4096 / 4096 | 100.13 | 531.80 | 5.31x |

The ggml Q8_0 kernel is the fastest CPU configuration at every shape: 3.2x its own F32, and a few
percent under OpenBLAS. The RK3576's CPU cores are ARMv8.0-A (`/proc/cpuinfo` has no `asimddp` and
no `asimdhp`), so they have no SDOT and no fp16 arithmetic. A CPU with the dot-product extension
would close some of this gap.

The ratio rises with M because a fixed cost amortizes. At K=N=2048 over four values of M, the
entry fits `T(M) = 10.9 + 0.0337*M` ms. The intercept is M-independent work: B's cube pack plus
the per-call BO allocation and teardown. It is 39% of the M=512 wall-clock time and 14% of the
M=2048 one.

The entry re-packs B on every call. The resident-weight entry
`rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_perc_wbo()` packs B once into a device BO
([../perf/rk3576-w8a8-llm.md](../perf/rk3576-w8a8-llm.md#supplied-sums-the-resident-weight-cube-and-the-bo-pool)).
At 512×2048×2048 its call measures 12.5-13.0 ms with the BO pool off [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9,
`rocket` 1.6.0, 786 MHz, 2026-09-25] (§"Cost at a prefill's M").

Weighted by a model's MAC shares, the ratio is smaller. A Llama-3.2-1B-shaped layer at ubatch 512
has MAC shares of 17% for attention QKVO, 55% for gate/up and 28% for down. With those weights,
and the down-projection left on the CPU because the entry declines its K (§"The K-split wedge"),
one layer is 2.39x, not 5.4x [host-computed, 2026-08-05]. That figure covers the GEMMs alone.
A second weighting of the GEMMs on 2026-08-10 gave 2.44x, and with a W8A8 path's host rotation
and quantization added, 2.37x
([../perf/rk3576-w8a8-llm.md](../perf/rk3576-w8a8-llm.md#host-cost-of-the-rotation-and-quantization)).
**Not characterized:** what separates the two GEMM-only weightings, 2.39x and 2.44x.

#### Cost at M = 1 and the decode projection

At M = 1 the call costs its M-independent intercept, so the transient entry is not a decode
engine. About 94% of an M = 1 call is machinery that a resident-weight path would not run: the
weight pack and the BO round trip.

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, kernel 7.1.7 with `rocket` 1.6.0 in-tree, the `ondemand` governor,
measured 2026-08-18/19.

| shape | NPU ms | CPU ms | ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| matmul 1×1024×1024 | 3.88 | 0.43 | 0.11x |
| matmul 1×2048×1024 | 8.72 | 0.78 | 0.09x |
| matmul 1×1536×1536 | 8.25 | 0.94 | 0.11x |
| matmul 32×2048×1024 | 7.85 | 24.63 | 3.14x |

[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 7.1.7 / `rocket` 1.6.0 in-tree, `ondemand`, 2026-08-18/19]

At the same K and N, M = 1 tracks M = 32 within 3-11%, which measures the intercept a second
way. The profiler `ROCKET_MM_PROFILE` splits the whole call into buckets: the entry's setup, its
BO teardown and its own entry-spanning time. At 1×2048×1024 the buckets measure:

| Bucket | ms/call |
|---|---:|
| `packB` | 3.27 |
| `alloc` | 2.36 |
| `bofree` | 1.18 |
| submit plus fence wait | ~0.27 |
| everything else | ~0 |

[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 7.1.7 / `rocket` 1.6.0 in-tree, `ondemand`, 2026-08-18/19]

The machinery is the pack and the five-BO create/mmap/munmap/GEM_CLOSE round trip. The packB cache
and the BO pool that remove it are in
[../perf/rk3576-w8a8-llm.md](../perf/rk3576-w8a8-llm.md#supplied-sums-the-resident-weight-cube-and-the-bo-pool). The teardown term is M-independent:
1.18 ms/call at M = 32 too, and 0.91 at 1×1024×1024. The write guard's read and every PREP/FINI
pair are inside other buckets.

The device-side remainder follows a weight-fetch line. The fence-wait marginal between the
1.05 MB and 2.10 MB rows is 86 us/MB, to about +-60 us/MB. The charsiu project fits 84.3 us/MB on
a ROCK 4D through its own kernel (`github.com/gahingwoo/charsiu`, chained tasks, 2026-08-15). The
two are the same regime, not the same number.

Decode cost with the transient machinery gone is a projection [expected], untested end to end.
A resident, chained M = 1 path costs the bytes term: an int8 model's weights once per token at
~86 us/MB. A CPU GEMV has the same DRAM cost on the RK3576, where one A72 thread saturates
memory. With int8 weights, NPU decode therefore projects to parity with the CPU rather than a
gain. Halving the bytes (int4) is the optimization that would move it.

The charsiu project reaches the same conclusion from the vendor side. The vendor runtime runs
M = 1 NPU decode at ~13 tokens/s on Llama-3.2-1B (their measurement, their board, their kernel).

### W8A8 LLM prefill

`ggml-rocket`'s W8A8 path drives the int8 matmul entry from stock llama.cpp. On Qwen2.5-1.5B it
runs prefill at 1.02x to 1.31x the CPU's Q8_0 kernel, and at 2.01x to 2.37x the same F16 GGUF on
the CPU. Its wikitext-2 perplexity is 1.011x the CPU's [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0,
2026-08-11 and 2026-08-23]. The path needs a Hadamard rotation and a per-column requant to reach that accuracy.
[../perf/rk3576-w8a8-llm.md](../perf/rk3576-w8a8-llm.md) gives the port, its accuracy, its
calibration and its conditions.

### The precision fork: what the op library should be founded on here

The matmul-form fp16 program, its accumulation, its bf16 form and its single-task limits are in
§"The fp16 matmul program".

The RK3588's composed op library is built on the fp16 matmul. It comprises:

- The norms
- Softmax
- The gated FFN
- Attention
- The ViT/Whisper encoder blocks

That foundation does not port to the RK3576, and the choice of what to put in its place is a
decision rather than a port. `librocketnpu`'s fp16 convolution program is not a matmul precision
here. One int8 task contracts `ic*kh*kw <= 4608`, and one task of that fp16 program contracts
exactly sixteen input channels. The same K therefore costs one submit against 288, and the
measured ratio runs 30x to 299x over ordinary shapes. Anything that lowers to that program
inherits the ratio. The sixteen channels are the convolution program's bound, not the SoC's,
because the matmul-form program contracts K in one task.

#### Cost against the int8 entry

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, NPU 786 MHz, both CPU clusters at governor
`performance`, 2026-09-25 UTC.

On the device, the fp16 program costs what int8 costs at N 64, and more at K 2048 N 256. From
submit to fence, with its buffers packed once, the program takes 0.076-0.084 ms to K 2048 at
N 64, the int8 entry's per-submit cost there. At K 2048 N 256 it takes 0.12-0.19 ms. In one
process at those two shapes, the fp16 program's device time is 0.122 ms at 1×2048×256 and
0.173 ms at 16×2048×256. The whole pooled int8 resident call is 0.102 ms and 0.136 ms there,
device time included. The fp16 weight is twice the int8 weight's bytes, which is the likely
cause [hypothesis]. [HW sweep]

Paired in one process against `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_perc_wbo()`, the resident int8 entry
an LLM's GEMMs call, the resident fp16 entry measures [HW sweep]:

| Shape (M×K×N) | fp16 resident | int8 resident | Ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 14 shapes to K 2048 at N 64, or K 512 at N 256 | 0.23-0.26 ms | 0.26-0.29 ms | 0.86-0.94x |
| 1×2048×256 | 0.32 ms | 0.30 ms | 1.07x |
| 16×2048×64 | 0.43 ms | 0.38 ms | 1.15x |
| 16×2048×256 | 0.59 ms | 0.40 ms | 1.50x |

The ratios above 1x come from the device time above, plus larger per-call buffers. The fp16
activation is twice the bytes, and its output four times. What is left per call is buffer
allocation, which fits as about 0.03 ms a BO plus 9 us a 4 KiB page. With
`ROCKET_RK3576_BO_POOL=1` and one entry per process, the resident call sits 0.014-0.043 ms above
its device time at every shape. [HW sweep]

> [!NOTE]
> A pooled cost depends on the pool's history. A pooled BO keeps the size it was allocated at,
> and a request takes the smallest free BO that covers it. After the mix of entries or shapes
> changes, a small request can hold a far larger buffer. The `PREP_BO` and `FINI_BO` ioctls
> sync the whole BO every call.

Five entries sharing one pool put the fp16 resident call 0.09-0.16 ms above its device time at 9
of 18 shapes, not monotone in any size. The resident entry alone, whose three buffers are all
small, stays at the single-entry figure above at all 18. The per-call entry alone still steps,
from 0.147 to 0.185 ms between its first two shapes. By the matching arithmetic, its 8 KiB weight
request is served by a 257 KiB BO the coefficients left [hypothesis]. [HW sweep, same board,
2026-09-25 UTC]

#### Cost at a prefill's M

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, NPU 786 MHz, governor `performance`, host on the
A72s, 2026-09-25 UTC. Both entries are resident and paired within a round, with the BO pool off.

Past one task the fp16 call costs about twice the int8 call, and the gap does not shrink as M
grows [HW sweep]:

| Shape (M×K×N) | fp16 resident | int8 resident | Ratio |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 16×1536×1536 | 1.06 ms | 0.67-0.71 ms | 1.5-1.6x |
| 64×1536×1536 | 2.44-2.46 ms | 1.25-1.26 ms | 2.0x |
| 128×1536×1536 and 256×1536×1536 | 4.7 and 9.1 ms | 2.2 and 4.1-4.2 ms | 2.2x |
| 512×1536×1536 | 17.5-17.8 ms | 8.5-8.7 ms | 2.0-2.1x |
| 512×1536×256 | 7.4-7.6 ms | 4.1 ms | 1.8-1.9x |
| 512×1536×4096 | 37.6-38.2 ms | 18.0-19.3 ms | 2.0-2.1x |
| 512×1536×8192 | 84.4-85.5 ms | 32.3-32.6 ms | 2.6x |
| 512×256×1536 | 11.7-11.8 ms | 4.3 ms | 2.7x |
| 512×4096×1536 | 39.0-39.6 ms | 20.5-21.1 ms | 1.9-2.0x |
| 512×6144×1536 | 86.2-86.4 ms | 87.1-90.3 ms | 1.0x |
| 512×2048×2048 | 24.6-24.7 ms | 12.5-13.0 ms | 1.9-2.0x |

The device time sets the ratio. At 512×1536×1536 the fp16 job takes 4.5 ms from submit to fence,
and the int8 entry's four submits take 2.1 ms by its own profile. Each fp16 task re-reads the
whole weight, and at that shape the program contracts at about half the int8 program's rate. The
two tie at K 6144 because the int8 entry slows there, while the fp16 program does not speed up.

The rest of the fp16 call is its per-call buffers. Allocating and freeing them is 7.2 ms of the
17.5-17.8. With `ROCKET_RK3576_BO_POOL=1`, one entry a process, the fp16 call is 6.9 ms against
the int8 call's 4.8, 1.4x. [HW sweep]

#### The int8 route's host terms

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, NPU 786 MHz, Qwen2.5-1.5B F16 on llama.cpp b10356,
`pp2048 -ub 512`, four A72 threads, governor `performance`, BO pool off, 2026-09-25.

An fp16 path would skip three host passes the int8 path makes around every GEMM: rotating and
quantizing the activation, and dequantizing the output. It would add one, narrowing the
activation to fp16. The frontend profiler measures each per GEMM shape, inside the model
[HW sweep]:

| M 512, K 1536, N | Projections | Calls reusing the previous activation | `quant+rot` | `dequant` | fp16 narrowing |
|---|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 1536 | q, o | none | 7.48-7.56 ms | 0.90-0.91 ms | 0.50 ms |
| 256 | k, v | all | 7.48-7.56 ms | 0.152 ms | 0.50 ms |
| 8960 | gate, up | half | 7.39-7.60 ms | 5.32-5.41 ms | 0.50 ms |

`quant+rot` depends on M·K alone, about 9.5 ns an element on the one thread that dispatches.
The entry measures 1-10% above its resident cost from the standalone library measurement, and
the processes measure 12.70-12.83 t/s.

Path against path, the int8 path is those host terms plus the resident int8 call. The fp16 path
is the narrowing plus the resident fp16 call. The fp16 entry takes N up to 8192, so N 8960 is
costed as two calls of N 4480. The fp16 device time grows faster than N: 11.3 ms at N 4480 and
35.7 ms at N 8192. Resident costs are measured one entry a process.

| Shape | int8 path, pool off | int8 path, pool on | fp16 path, pool off | fp16 path, pool on | fp16/int8, pool off | fp16/int8, pool on |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 512×1536×1536 | 17.0 ms | 13.2 ms | 18.4 ms | 7.4 ms | 1.08x | 0.56x |
| 512×1536×256 | 11.7 ms | 9.5 ms | 8.1 ms | 3.0 ms | 0.69x | 0.32x |
| 512×1536×8960 | 50.9 ms | 41.9 ms | 81.5 ms | 34.5 ms | 1.60x | 0.82x |
| One layer, two of each | 159 ms | 129 ms | 216 ms | 90 ms | 1.36x | 0.70x |

[HW sweep, same board and date]

The host terms decide the pooled comparison and not the unpooled one. At 512×1536×1536 they come
to 7.9 ms net of the narrowing. The host terms exceed the pooled fp16 excess of 2.1 ms and fall
below the unpooled one of 9.3 ms. Unpooled, the fp16 call is its buffers: 29 of its 40.5 ms at
N 4480.

In prefill wall-clock time the comparison is small. The offloaded GEMMs are about 17.4 s of the
160 s a `pp2048` takes here, 11%, with the pool off. Pooled, an fp16 path takes 4.4 s off, 2.7%
of the wall-clock time, and unpooled it adds 6.1 s [host-computed].

Half of the pooled 4.4 s is repeat work. The k and v projections rotate and quantize q's
activation again, and up does gate's. Quantizing each activation once removes 2.5 s by the same
arithmetic. The rest is single-threaded host work beside three idle A72s [expected].

**Not characterized:** four terms the comparison leaves out:

- Accuracy (the int8 path is 1.011x perplexity)
- An fp16 K split for `ffn_down` (K 8960 is past the fp16 entry's 6144)
- Another model or `-ub`
- Calibration

The int8 path calibrates on its first two forward passes, 21.9-22.1 s a process here. A
`llama-bench` warm-up hides that time, and a process's first prompt incurs it. An fp16 path has
no calibration.

#### Outcome of the precision fork

The fork does not turn on speed. The int8 path's host terms exceed the fp16 GEMM's extra cost
only with the BO pool on. Even then the fp16 path saves 2.7% of Qwen2.5-1.5B's prefill
wall-clock time, and half of that is removable on the int8 side. At M 16 the matmul form costs
0.86-1.15x the int8 resident entry and needs no power-cycle guard. At a prefill's M its GEMM
costs about twice the int8 one.

The int8 matmul serves the GEMMs but not the reductions. The entry `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576()`
is fast and bit-exact, but its output goes through the DPU's requant and is int8. The RK3588
builds RMSNorm, softmax's row sums and every ReduceMean as a matmul by a ones vector. A reduction
of that kind needs an accumulator wider than its operands, and an int8 output saturates on the
first long row. The 32-bit entry, `rocket_matmul_int8_rk3576_i32()`, runs on the dense writer
(§"A whole output stage that does not poison"). It does not corrupt the next submit, idles only
on a redo, and one task writes a 38 KiB output exactly.

The older int32 writers are the wrong instrument for a per-layer norm for three reasons, and the
dense writer removes two of them. The older writers corrupt the next submit and need a power
cycle for it, and their output is bounded at 8 KiB a task. The matmul test's 21 int32 shapes are
exact on the dense writer in 0.26 s, against 6.3 s on the older ones
(`ROCKET_RK3576_I32_DENSE=0`). [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, rocket 1.6.0, 2026-09-30]

> [!CAUTION]
> On the narrow int32 writer, the shape M 512 K 8192 N 2048 takes the SoC down until a reboot.
> Software must run that shape on the dense writer, where it completes in 345.7 ms and 48 submits
> with no redo. Its 4096 sampled elements are exact [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, rocket 1.6.0,
> 2026-09-30].

The third reason is not characterized on the dense writer. The older writers drop atoms at a rate
the memory system sets, so the library keeps its sentinel scan.

On the RK3576 the transformer composition stays on the CPU, and the NPU runs four kinds of op:

- Convolution
- int8 GEMM
- Pooling
- LUT activations

That decision rests in part on the fp16 cost above, which the int8 host terms do not repay.

The three operations that support the decision are all on the convolution and DPU side rather
than the matmul side. The direct and depthwise int8 convolutions and the int8 first conv are
bit-exact and 10-76x a scalar CPU reference. The PPU gives pooling as an independent 31-write
program in the cube the conv path already packs, and the DPU LUT gives the nonlinear activations.
Together they form a CNN, detection and classification stack, the workload this silicon is
shaped for.

All four parts that decision named are implemented and tested:

- Per-output-channel weight scales on the direct and depthwise paths and through a graph
- The PPU pooling emitter
- The DPU LUT and its activation entry
- A whole network

### A whole network

MobileNetV1-224 at int8 runs end to end on the RK3576 and returns TFLite's own label. The graph
is 27 convolutions, a PPU pooling layer and a classifier. Every one of the 29 compute layers is
bit-exact against a CPU model of the RK3576's arithmetic. The whole graph's wall-clock time per
inference depends on which optimizations are in place:

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, MobileNetV1-224 int8. Each row is its own measurement, taken as its
optimization was added, from 2026-07-29 (the bottom row) to 2026-08-03 (the top row).

| Configuration | Wall-clock time per inference |
|---|---:|
| The pooling layer inside the kick, so the whole graph is one kick in one submit | **4.9-5.0 ms** |
| Resident weights, tensors kept in cube layout between layers, each chained run of cube-joined layers as one hardware kick, and the pooling layer resident, reading its input as a cube and retiring on the PPU's own completion | 5.1 ms |
| The same with the pooling layer transient, scattering its input on the host, allocating its buffers per call and retiring at the `dpu_grace_us` deadline | 6.2 ms |
| One submit per layer | 10.4-10.5 ms |
| The transposes at every layer boundary | 21 ms |
| No resident weights at all | 98.8 ms |

The sections below take that difference apart and measure each part of it.

#### The graph planner

The placement and join passes are in `graph/rocket_graph_rk3576.c`:

- One buffer per add and per concatenation, with a slice per operand
- The declared tails
- The shared surfaces
- The adjacent-pair join, with its refusal buckets
- The caller of the chained-run finder

The passes run over a frontend-neutral layer description:

- Kind
- Geometry
- The operand graph, by producer
- The resident handles
- One flag for the thing no rule there can derive: whether the caller prepares that layer's
  input on the host

The planner's caller holds the rest:

- The weights
- The tensors
- The ping-pong
- The skip lifetimes
- The de-tile points

The planner is a separate target that the frontends link. It is not part of `librocketnpu`,
which holds no graph-execution object. The chained-run finder (`rocket_chain_plan_rk3576()`) is in
the library because it is pure, and it is the one part a frontend cannot hold itself.

Twelve refusal classes are closed, and every one is a host-buffer rule rather than a hardware
rule. The frontends share the planner rather than copy it, because a second copy would diverge
where being wrong is silent. A frontend that uses the planner gets every row of the
MobileNetV1-224 table above. A frontend that calls only the op entries gets about a twentieth of
the demonstrated throughput. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

#### The TFLite delegate

A second caller runs the planner: the TFLite delegate, `tflite-rocket`, on real `.tflite` files.
It lowers a delegated partition onto the same neutral description and runs the plan. An
unmodified `.tflite` therefore reaches the RK3576 through the ordinary external-delegate API,
rather than only through a hand-built blob.

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, kernel 7.1.6, NPU 786 MHz, warm, on a real image, 2026-08-04. The
delegate column includes the interpreter's own invoke and the transposes at the partition
boundaries. The CPU column is the same interpreter with no delegate. The last three columns are
the nodes claimed, the adjacent-pair joins and the hardware kicks that the plan produced.

| model | delegate | CPU | claimed | joins | kicks |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| MobileNetV1-224 | 5.2 ms | 71.7 | 29/31 | 28/28 | 1 |
| MobileNetV2-224 | 6.8 ms | 45.3 | 64/66 | 63/63 | 1 |
| ResNet-18-224 | 8.8 ms | 167.1 | 36/38 | 27/27 | 1 |
| Inception V1-224 | 7.4 ms | 180.5 | 81/83 | 53/53 | 1 |
| Inception V3-299 | 34.5 ms | 669.3 | 130/132 | 90/94 | 16 |
| SSD-MobileNetV2-COCO | 17.0 ms | 109.0 | 87/111 | 70/82 | 6 |
| EfficientDet-Lite0-320 | 55.5 ms | 123.1 | 238/267 | not re-read | not re-read |

The EfficientDet-Lite0 row is from 2026-09-27, on `rocket` 1.6.0 and kernel 7.2.3 [HW sweep,
H96 MAX M9]. Both CPU clusters were pinned to `performance`, and the per-axis gain was in the BS
shift word (§"Per-axis gain on the BS shift word"). No layer is refused, and the 29 unclaimed
nodes are op kinds the delegate does not claim. Its joins and kicks were not re-read on that
build.

Every classifier returns the interpreter's own top-1. The delegate attempts the packed-image stem
only at a zero input zero point, where the output stays a cube.

#### Claim-time refusals

A `DEPTHWISE_CONV_2D` of four or fewer channels has no form on the RK3576. The packed-image first
conv folds the kernel's columns into the channel axis, so nothing is left to be depthwise over.
The library refuses `direct_datapath` on a depthwise descriptor rather than ignoring it.
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

The detectors contain two node kinds that no hand-built graph produces: that depthwise
convolution, and a `QUANTIZE` on a narrow tensor. The `QUANTIZE` meets the same limit through its
lowering, since the requantization edge's natural form is a depthwise 1×1 identity. On four or
fewer channels it takes the direct identity instead, which is the same arithmetic and sixteen
weights. An SSD box-encoding branch has exactly both shapes. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

The delegate must answer both at claim time. A layer the library will not pack is a Prepare
failure, and a Prepare failure fails the whole model. The framework runs an unclaimed node
itself. [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]

A claim-time refusal is cheaper than a late one by more than a prepare. Before it claims a node,
the delegate checks every bound that the library enforces and that the shape decides:

- The pooling plan
- The per-axis gain ramp
- Through `rocket_conv2d_int8_plan_rk3576()`, the convolution geometry, the resident weight
  slice and the row window

> [!NOTE]
> The pack never reaches the row window, and only the run's per-task plan does. A model past
> the row window builds cleanly, passes `AllocateTensors`, and throws at every `Invoke`. That
> failure comes later than a Prepare failure, and past any load-time validation a caller can do.

#### Detector accuracy

**Conditions:** mAP@[.5:.95] over 500 COCO val2017 images, from the same interpreter with and
without the delegate, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04. EfficientDet-Lite0's per-axis layers run on the
shift-0 ramp, the `C` ramp with the BS shift word at zero (`ROCKET_RK3576_PC_SHIFT=0`), with
`ROCKET_RK3576_PC_INERT` at its default. With the gain in the BS shift word, the default per-axis
path, it scores 0.2818 (§"Per-axis gain on the BS shift word").

Both detectors score at CPU parity:

| model | delegate | CPU | delta |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| SSD-MobileNetV2 | 0.2617 | 0.2631 | -0.0014 |
| EfficientDet-Lite0 | 0.2826 | 0.2823 | +0.0003 |

> [!NOTE]
> A detector's output is an NMS-ordered list, so one count of arithmetic drift reorders or drops
> a box. An element-wise diff against the CPU therefore says nothing about a detector's accuracy,
> and mAP is the comparison a detector admits.

#### Per-channel C minimum

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, per-axis ResNet-18 and EfficientDet-Lite0 through the TFLite
delegate, on the shift-0 ramp, 2026-08-04. On the default per-axis path, the BS shift word sets
each task's base gain from its largest live scale. Only a live channel below 1/65534 of that
scale clamps there (§"Per-axis gain on the BS shift word").

The per-channel `C` has a minimum of one, and below it a channel computes at the tile's gain
instead of its own. A tile is one task, and a task has one `OUT_CVT` shift. A channel therefore
reaches its own scale through the int16 `C` in its coefficient group and nothing else. The base
gain is already the smallest the tile admits, and every channel's `C` must fit that channel's
own accumulator bound. A channel whose scale sits below half the base therefore needs a `C`
under one, and the field's minimum is one.

> [!CAUTION]
> A channel whose `C` clamps at one is wrong by a factor, and its relative gain error exceeds
> 100%. The output is the correct size with plausible values, and no error is reported.
> Software must find every clamped channel before it submits a per-axis layer.

The packer's log measures 311% and 8150% on two per-axis ResNet-18 layers, and past 100% on 7 of
EfficientDet's 182 per-channel layers. The clamp is the ramp's own boundary rather than a chosen
threshold. Above it, `C` is an integer and the resolution is `0.5/C`, which the tile search
improves at the cost of more submits. Below it there is no `C` to choose.

A clamped channel is exactly one whose relative gain error exceeds 100%, and the library refuses
its layer unless the channel's output cannot vary (§"Inert clamped channels"). The pure call
`rocket_conv2d_int8_perchannel_plan_rk3576()` answers that question. The delegate asks it at
claim time, where a refusal costs one node that the framework runs itself. The median
EfficientDet layer is 0.021%.

The depthwise path is the one affected. A depthwise layer is one task at any channel count, so
its tile cannot be narrowed. Of the 7 clamped layers, 6 are depthwise (1152, 1152, 1152, 672,
240, 32) and 1 is direct: the stem, already at the emitter's minimum of 32. With
`ROCKET_RK3576_PC_ALLOW_CLAMP=1` the layer runs anyway, at a cost of 0.0428 mAP on EfficientDet:
0.2395 against the CPU's 0.2823, where refusing scores 0.2809.

#### Inert clamped channels

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, EfficientDet-Lite0 through the TFLite delegate, 500 COCO val2017
images per configuration, the shift-0 ramp, 2026-08-04. The default per-axis path puts the gain
in the BS shift word and programs every channel whose output cannot vary as its byte. Its time
and mAP are in §"Per-axis gain on the BS shift word".

A clamped channel in these models is a pruned one. Each of the 111 clamped channels in the six
depthwise layers, and each of the stem's four, has an all-zero filter. An all-zero tensor is
quantized against the quantizer's minimum scale (TFLite's 1e-6). The channel's scale therefore
sits four to five orders of magnitude below its neighbors', while its int32 bias still has the
layer's magnitude. A scale that far below its neighbors' puts the channel below half the tile's
base gain.

A clamp is a defect only where it changes the output byte. A channel with no live weight
produces one accumulator value, the zero-point fold `A`, at every output position. Its MAC term
is zero for any feature cube. Whether such a channel's output byte is correct is therefore
decidable exactly, with no bound and no sampling.

For 105 of those 111, the fold is large and negative, and saturates to -128 under either gain.
The library's rule tests saturation rather than equality: both gains must drive the fold past
the same saturation limit by more than one count. The answer then does not depend on the DPU's
rounding, which the rule models on the host rather than running it. A clamped channel with a
live weight is always refused. Its accumulator ranges over `+-128*sum|w|` around `A`, and a gain
wrong by a factor is wrong somewhere in that range.

The rule takes EfficientDet-Lite0 from 7 refusals to 4. It accepts the stem, the first
depthwise layer and the 240-channel layer. On their pruned channels, all three are exact
against an exact per-axis requant [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04]. Their non-clamped
channels are all within 0.077-0.097% of their own gain.

Accepting the clamped channels raises the detector's score slightly, and their per-axis
arithmetic costs no accuracy. With them refused, EfficientDet-Lite0 scores 0.2809 against the
CPU's 0.2823. With them accepted on the depthwise path only, it scores 0.2832, and with the
default full acceptance, 0.2826 [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04].

`ROCKET_RK3576_PC_INERT` selects the acceptance, and is read only on the shift-0 ramp. Its default
is full acceptance, `0` refuses every clamped channel, and `dw` restricts the acceptance to the
depthwise path.

A refused layer splits the delegated partition it sits in, so its cost depends on the graph
rather than on the node. With the clamped channels refused, EfficientDet-Lite0's head is a
12-layer and a 28-layer partition, with 5394 KiB of cube joins between them. With them
accepted, the head is one 43-layer partition with 7594 KiB. The claimed-node counts barely
change (231, 233, 234 of 267), and inference time changes by 17%:

| Setting | claimed | head partition | inference time | vs CPU TFLite |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|
| `PC_INERT=0` | 231 | 12 + 28 layers, 5394 KiB joined | 99.3 ms | 1.23x |
| `PC_INERT=dw` | 233 | 42 layers, 6794 KiB | 90.3 ms | 1.35x |
| **default (full)** | **234** | **43 layers, 7594 KiB** | **82.8 ms** | **1.47x** |

**Conditions:** CPU governor pinned to `performance` [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04].

Inference time separates the two acceptance modes, and the mAP does not. Their 7.5 ms
difference is twelve times the run-to-run spread. Their 0.0006 mAP difference is inside what
one count of drift does to an NMS-ordered detection list [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04].

#### Channel-range splits

On the shift-0 ramp, splitting a depthwise layer into channel ranges removes the clamp from 3 of
the 6 clamped depthwise layers [host-computed, 2026-08-04]. A depthwise convolution is one task
because output channel `c` is bound to input channel `c`. A split gives each channel range its own
task and its own `OUT_CVT` shift. Each range is a multiple of the output cube's 16-channel atom.

The split clears op#15 at a uniform tile of 96, and ops #44 and #52 at 32 and 16. A
non-uniform partition clears them at 3, 6 and 3 programs, built greedy-longest from the left,
which a planner can emit. The other three each contain an irreducible 16-channel atom: one that
holds both the channel that sets the base gain and the smallest-scale channel. No 16-aligned
partition clears them at all. One of them (op#48) gets worse as the tile shrinks, with 1
clamped range at 1152 against 4 at 32 [host-computed, 2026-08-04].

A depthwise layer cannot reorder its channels. A consecutive range therefore narrows the scale
spread only where the model's channel order happens to be locally scale-ordered. Whether a
split helps depends on the model, not on the tiling.

#### Channel order of a per-axis output

The direct per-axis path of `librocketnpu` sorts a layer's output channels by scale. Each
output-channel tile then spans as little of the layer's scale range as the tiling allows. A
tile's channels occupy consecutive slots of the output. A sorted handle therefore writes its
output channels in scale order, not in the model's order. The host de-tile puts a
materialized tensor back in model order, so a per-op result that is materialized shows no sign
of the sort.

> [!CAUTION]
> A layer that reads its input as a cube takes the channel order as stored. Fed a scale-sorted
> output, it produces output of the correct size with incorrect values, and reports no error.
> Software must hand the next layer a cube in the model's channel order.

With one output-channel tile, the sort changes nothing: that tile spans every channel in any
order. The base gain and each channel's `C` are the same values in different slots. The
single-tile path therefore does not sort, and writes its output in model order. Multi-tile
handles keep the sort, which is where their accuracy comes from. Their cube is refused in any
case, because each tile has its own output BO. `r76_cube_shape_ok()` also checks the
invariant, where the cube join depends on it.

With both per-axis defects handled, per-axis ResNet-18 agrees with the CPU's top-1 on 89 of 100
images, against the per-tensor build's 93 of 100. EfficientDet is at CPU parity. The
remaining gap is the per-axis requant's own accuracy on near-ties, which is the expected regime
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, 2026-08-04].

#### Per-axis gain on the BS shift word

**Conditions:** H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, EfficientDet-Lite0 through the TFLite delegate,
2026-09-27.

As on the RK3588, the BS shift word contains a shift field for each sign
([rk3576-regcmd.md §"DPU shift operand (`0x5024`)"](rk3576-regcmd.md#dpu-shift-operand-0x5024))
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9].

> [!CAUTION]
> A shift programmed in one sign field alone scales the other sign's products wrongly, and the
> output has the correct size and incorrect values. Software must program the shift in both
> sign fields.

`librocketnpu` programs the per-axis gain in the BS shift word. Each task's largest live scale
takes `C` = 32767. The BS shift is the smallest at which `((acc + A)*C) >> s` fits int32 at
every live channel's accumulator bound. A channel whose exact output is one byte over its whole
reachable accumulator is programmed as that byte, through its own `A` and `C`.

On EfficientDet-Lite0 those constant-byte channels number 345 over 28 layers: all-zero filters,
and dead channels driven past the saturation limit. They are every channel that the shift-0
ramp, the `C` ramp with the shift word at zero, clamps. Only a live channel below 1/65534 of its
task's largest live scale is refused. The per-axis path is bit-exact against the extended host
model [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9, `rocket` 1.6.0, 2026-09-27].

Under the shift-0 ramp, the output-channel split costs more than the refusals. The ramp refuses
4 of EfficientDet-Lite0's 182 per-axis layers and cuts 11 direct layers into 133 tiles. A
multi-tile layer cannot produce a cube, so 6 joins are refused too. Of an 83.1 ms inference, the
refusals cost 6.3 ms and the tiles 20.1 ms.

With the gain in the shift word, no layer is refused or split. With both CPU clusters pinned to
`performance`, the model runs in 55.5 ms against 83.6 (0.661x paired), 2.22x the CPU's 123.1 ms
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]. Its mAP@[.5:.95] is 0.2818 against the CPU's 0.2823 over 500 val2017
images. Setting `ROCKET_RK3576_PC_SHIFT=0` restores the shift-0 ramp. Its detections are then
byte-identical to those of a build without the shift word.

#### Distance from TFLite

**Conditions:** MobileNetV1-224, per-tensor int8, H96 MAX M9.

The DPU's `OUT_CVT` converter and TFLite's requant are fixed-point approximations of one
real-valued scale, with different multiplier widths and tie rules
([../encodings/out-cvt-converter.md](../encodings/out-cvt-converter.md#the-cvt_round-tie-rule)).
They therefore disagree on the elements near a rounding boundary.

Against TFLite the graph differs on 0.095% of elements, every one of them by a single count,
and that difference is the requant's. The pooling layer and the classifier match exactly. When
each layer takes its own previous output rather than TFLite's, the disagreements compound down
the graph. At the classifier 76% of the logits are bit-identical, and the top-1 and the whole
top-5 still agree.

The fused RELU6 costs nothing. Every intermediate tensor is quantized at scale 6/255 with its
zero point at the bottom of the range. The activation's clamp is therefore exactly the int8
saturation the DPU already applies.

#### CBUF allowances, SAME padding and the stem

A whole network depends on three rules that per-op shapes do not reach. The first is that the CNA's
trailing pad is the pad its last window consumes, derived from the output extent. TFLite's
asymmetric SAME padding is therefore programmed as an output extent, with no border copied into the
cube chain ("Asymmetric padding as an output extent", above).

> [!CAUTION]
> On the depthwise path, the two low CBUF allowance settings are not honored. A task whose
> window selects one writes incorrect output past the budget it receives, and reports no
> error. Software must not select either setting on the depthwise path.

On the direct path, whether those settings are honored depends on the resident weight
footprint. On the depthwise path no footprint threshold fits at all, and the library declines
both settings. See "The symmetry audit on the CNA" and "On the DEPTHWISE path the two low CBUF
rungs do not deliver", above.

The int8 first convolution on a packed image has bounds that together encode the ONNX
symmetric-SAME convention. A TFLite SAME stem has `pad_left = 0`, so it does not meet them
directly. MobileNetV1-224 therefore runs its stem on the direct path, at the model's own three
channels. The direct path takes three channels because the int8 direct cube is a 32-channel MAC
group at every channel count ("Narrow input-channel counts on the direct datapath", above).

Widening the stem to eight channels is also exact. The added channels hold the input zero point
against zero weights. Such channels add nothing to either half of the coefficient `A` fold, for
any zero point.

The stem has a second lowering. Shifting the input by one pixel against a pad of one moves the
sample grid by a whole stride. The shifted grid reproduces TFLite's bit-exactly in the interior.
That lowering is implemented and tested, and it is slower than a resident widened stem ("TFLite
stem through an input shift", above).

#### Inference time with per-call weight packing

**Conditions:** MobileNetV1-224, H96 MAX M9, weights packed on every call, a transpose at every
layer boundary, 40 submits of one task each, 2026-07-29.

Every layer of the graph runs on paths with no wide-output hazard. The graph is int8
throughout, so it needs neither the guard against a corrupted next submit nor a power cycle. The
atom drop that the int32 writers show did not appear. The measured inference time therefore
contains no redo and no power cycle [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9].

Under these conditions the inference time is host arithmetic over the weights, not dispatch.
The three layers that cost most, the stem and the two 1024-channel 1×1 convolutions, take 5, 1
and 1 of the 40 submits. Output-channel tiling is not what those layers spend time on: each is
one output-channel tile, and the stem's five submits are its row tasks ("Join pass", below).

`ROCKET_RK3576_INT8_PROF=1` splits a call into seven buckets:

- Sums
- Feature pack
- Weight pack
- Coefficients
- Plan+gen
- Submit
- De-tile

Across the graph the submit is 1-16% of a layer and 3% of the two most expensive, against a
host share of 84-99%. These jobs take 0.13-0.25 ms each. The ~340 us per-submit minimum applies
to a round trip that waits for a whole output to drain, and they are well under it.

> [!NOTE]
> A layer's submit count does not give its submit time. Measure the submit time before
> attributing a layer's cost to dispatch.

The sections below remove these terms. Resident weights remove the weight arithmetic, the cube
layout removes the transposes, and the kick removes most of the submits. With all three in
place, the host share is 6-12%, and the two remaining terms are the submit and the write guard
(see "What the wall is made of, once the graph work is collected").

The host work is two loops over the weight tensor, and neither depends on the input. The
per-output-channel filter sums fold the input zero point, and the cube scatter is a pure
re-layout. Straightening them in place is measured in "The host cube scatter", above.

#### Resident weight handles

**Conditions:** MobileNetV1-224, H96 MAX M9, 2026-07-29. Times are per inference. The per-call
and resident figures are paired on one build, measured after the straightening in "The host
cube scatter".

**Not characterized:** the difference between this per-call figure, 98.8 ms, and the 115 ms the
straightening measured earlier the same day.

`rocket_conv2d_int8_pack_rk3576()` returns a handle a caller packs once and passes per
inference. The two weight loops and the BO allocation and release they need are 65% of the
inference time. Taking them out of the inference moves it 98.8 ms -> 33.6 ms, a 2.94x speedup on
the same 40 submits [HW sweep, H96 MAX M9]. The result is bit-identical to the per-call entry's.
The graph's TFLite distance is the same 4792 of 5044713 elements. Each of the 29 compute layers stays bit-exact
against a CPU model of the SoC's arithmetic, and the whole top-5 is unchanged.

A transient call builds the same handle and packs each output-channel tile as its tile loop
reaches it. The per-call and resident entries are therefore one code path, with one arithmetic
[HW sweep, H96 MAX M9].

Only 29 ms of the 65 is the weight arithmetic the profile predicts. The BO teardown runs after
the profiler's last bucket. A profile not reconciled against the measured inference time
